</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Christian_mysticism" title="Template:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Christian_mysticism" title="Template talk:Christian mysticism"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Christian_mysticism&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Henry Suso</b>, OP (also called <b>Amandus</b>, a name adopted in his writings, and <b>Heinrich Seuse</b> or <b>Heinrich von Berg</b><sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> in <a href="/wiki/German_language" title="German language">German</a>; 21 March 1295 – 25 January 1366) was a <a href="/wiki/German_people" class="mw-redirect" title="German people">German</a> <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Order" title="Dominican Order">Dominican friar</a> and the most popular vernacular writer of the fourteenth century (when considering the number of surviving manuscripts). Suso is thought to have been born on 21 March 1295. An important author in both Latin and Middle High German, he is also notable for defending <a href="/wiki/Meister_Eckhart" title="Meister Eckhart">Meister Eckhart</a>'s legacy after Eckhart was posthumously condemned for heresy in 1329.<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> He died in <a href="/wiki/Free_Imperial_City_of_Ulm" title="Free Imperial City of Ulm">Ulm</a> on 25 January 1366, and was <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatified</a> by the Catholic Church in 1831.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Biography">Biography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_Suso&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Biography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Suso was born <b>Heinrich von Berg</b>, a member of the ruling family of <a href="/wiki/Berg_(state)" class="mw-redirect" title="Berg (state)">Berg</a>. He was born in either the <a href="/wiki/Free_imperial_city" title="Free imperial city">free imperial city</a> of <a href="/wiki/%C3%9Cberlingen" title="Überlingen">Überlingen</a> on <a href="/wiki/Lake_Constance" title="Lake Constance">Lake Constance</a> or nearby <a href="/wiki/Konstanz" title="Konstanz">Constance</a>, on 21 March 1295 (or perhaps on that date up to 1297–99).<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Later, out of humility and devotion to his mother, he took her family name, which was Sus (or Süs, meaning "sweet"). At 13 years of age he was admitted to the <a href="/wiki/Novitiate" title="Novitiate">novitiate</a> of the Dominican Order at their <a href="/wiki/Priory" title="Priory">priory</a> in <a href="/wiki/Konstanz" title="Konstanz">Constance</a>. After completing that year of probation, he advanced to do his preparatory, philosophical, and <a href="/wiki/Theology" title="Theology">theological</a> studies there.
</p><p>In the prologue to his <i>Life</i>, Suso recounts how, after about five years in the monastery (in other words, when he was about 18 years old), he experienced a conversion to a deeper form of <a href="/wiki/Religious_life" class="mw-redirect" title="Religious life">religious life</a> through the intervention of Divine Wisdom. He made himself "the Servant of Eternal Wisdom", which he identified with the divine essence and, in more specific terms, with divine Eternal Wisdom made man in Christ. From this point forward in his account of his spiritual life, a burning love for Eternal Wisdom dominated his thoughts and controlled his actions; his spiritual journey culminated in a <a href="/wiki/Bridal_theology" title="Bridal theology">mystical marriage</a> to Christ in the form of the <a href="/wiki/Sophia_(wisdom)#Christianity" title="Sophia (wisdom)">Eternal Wisdom</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> an allegorical Goddess in the Hebrew Bible associated with Christ in medieval devotion.<sup id="cite_ref-ebrsuso_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ebrsuso-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-wordimage_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wordimage-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> 
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Career">Career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_Suso&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Suso was then sent on for further studies in philosophy and theology, probably first at the Dominican monastery in <a href="/wiki/Strasbourg" title="Strasbourg">Strasbourg</a>, perhaps between 1319 and 1321, and then from 1324 to 1327 he took a supplementary course in theology in the Dominican <i><a href="/wiki/Studium_Generale" class="mw-redirect" title="Studium Generale">Studium Generale</a></i> in <a href="/wiki/Cologne" title="Cologne">Cologne</a>, where he would have come into contact with <a href="/wiki/Meister_Eckhart" title="Meister Eckhart">Meister Eckhart</a>, and probably also <a href="/wiki/Johannes_Tauler" title="Johannes Tauler">Johannes Tauler</a>, both celebrated mystics.<sup id="cite_ref-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.198_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.198-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Returning to his home priory at Constance in about 1327, Suso was appointed to the office of <i>lector</i> (lecturer). His teaching, however, aroused criticism – most likely because of his connection with Eckhart in the wake of the latter's trial and condemnation in 1326–29. Suso's <i>Little Book of Truth</i>, a short defence of Eckhart's teaching, probably dates from this time, perhaps 1329. In 1330 this treatise, and another, were denounced as heretical by enemies in the Order. Suso traveled to the Dominican <a href="/wiki/General_Chapter" class="mw-redirect" title="General Chapter">General Chapter</a> held at <a href="/wiki/Maastricht" title="Maastricht">Maastricht</a> in 1330 to defend himself. The consequence is not entirely known – at some point between 1329 and 1334 he was removed from his lectorship in Constance, though he was not personally condemned.<sup id="cite_ref-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.198_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.198-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Knowledge of Suso's activities in subsequent years is somewhat sketchy. It is known that he served as prior of the Constance convent – most likely between 1330 and 1334, though possibly in the 1340s.<sup id="cite_ref-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.198_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.198-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> It is also known that he had various devoted disciples, a group including both men and women, especially those connected to the <i><a href="/wiki/Friends_of_God" title="Friends of God">Friends of God</a></i> movement. His influence was especially strong in many religious communities of women, particularly in the Dominican Monastery of <a href="/wiki/Diessenhofen" title="Diessenhofen">St. Katharinental</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Thurgau" title="Thurgau">Thurgau</a>, a famous nursery of mysticism in the 13th and 14th centuries. In the mid-1330s, during his visits to various communities of Dominican <a href="/wiki/Nun" title="Nun">nuns</a> and <a href="/wiki/Beguines" class="mw-redirect" title="Beguines">Beguines</a>, Suso became acquainted with <a href="/wiki/Elsbeth_Stagel" title="Elsbeth Stagel">Elsbeth Stagel</a>, <a href="/wiki/Prioress" class="mw-redirect" title="Prioress">prioress</a> of the monastery of Dominican nuns in <a href="/wiki/T%C3%B6ss" title="Töss">Töss</a>. The two became close friends. She translated some of his Latin writings into German, collected and preserved most of his extant letters, and at some point began gathering the materials that Suso eventually put together into his <i>Life of the Servant</i>.
</p><p>Suso shared in the exile of the Dominican community from Constance between 1339 and 1346, during the most heated years of the quarrel originally between <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_XXII" title="Pope John XXII">Pope John XXII</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Louis_IV,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Louis IV, Holy Roman Emperor">Holy Roman Emperor</a>, continued by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XII" title="Pope Benedict XII">Pope Benedict XII</a>. He was transferred to the monastery at Ulm in about 1348. He seems to have remained there for the rest of his life. Here, during his final years (possibly 1361–63), he edited his four vernacular works into <i>The Exemplar</i>.
</p><p>Suso died in Ulm on 25 January 1366.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Mortifications">Mortifications</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_Suso&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Mortifications">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Early in his life, Suso subjected himself to extreme forms of mortifications; later on he reported that God told him they were unnecessary. During this period, Suso devised for himself several painful devices. Some of these were: an undergarment studded with a hundred and fifty brass nails, a very uncomfortable door to sleep on, and a cross with thirty protruding needles and nails under his body as he slept. In the autobiographical text in which he reports these, however, he ultimately concludes that they are unnecessary distractions from the love of God.<sup id="cite_ref-philosophy_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-philosophy-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings">Writings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_Suso&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Writings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Suso's first work was the <i>Büchlein der Wahrheit</i> (<i>Little Book of Truth</i>) written between 1328 and 1334 in <a href="/wiki/Konstanz" title="Konstanz">Constance</a>. This was a short defence of the teaching of Meister Eckhart, who had been tried for heresy and condemned in 1328–29. In 1330 this treatise and another (possibly the <i>Little Book of Eternal Wisdom</i>) were denounced as heretical by Dominican opponents, leading Suso to travel to the Dominican General Chapter held at Maastricht in 1330 to defend himself.<sup id="cite_ref-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.198_7-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.198-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Suso's next book, <i>Das Büchlein der ewigen Weisheit</i> (<i>The Little Book of Eternal Wisdom</i>), written around 1328–1330,<sup id="cite_ref-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.198_7-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.198-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> is less speculative and more practical. At some point between 1334 and 1337 Suso translated this work into Latin, but in doing so added considerably to its contents, and made of it an almost entirely new book, which he called the <i><a href="/wiki/Horologium_Sapientiae" title="Horologium Sapientiae">Horologium Sapientiae</a></i> (<a href="/wiki/Horologium_Sapientiae" title="Horologium Sapientiae"><i>Clock of Wisdom</i></a>). This book was dedicated to the new Dominican <a href="/wiki/Master_General_of_the_Dominican_Order" class="mw-redirect" title="Master General of the Dominican Order">Master General</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hugh_of_Vaucemain" title="Hugh of Vaucemain">Hugh of Vaucemain</a>, who appears to have been a supporter of his.<sup id="cite_ref-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.198_7-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.198-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At some point in the following decades, Stagel formed a collection of 28 of Suso's letters in the <i>Grosses Briefbuch</i> (<i>Great Book of Letters</i>), which survives. Suso also wrote a long text purporting to tell the story of his spiritual life and ascetic practices (variously referred to as the <i>Life of the Servant</i>, <i>Life</i>, <i>Vita</i>, or <i>Leben Seuses</i>), and revised the <i>Büchlein der Wahrheit</i> and the <i>Büchlein der ewigen Weisheit</i>. At some point in his later years, perhaps 1361–63, he collected these works, together with 11 of his letters (the <i>Briefbüchlein</i>, or <i>Little Book of Letters</i>, a selection of letters from the <i>Grosses Briefbuch</i>), and wrote a prologue, to form one book he referred to as <i>The Exemplar</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.204_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.204-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There are also various sermons attributed to Suso, although only two appear to be authentic.<sup id="cite_ref-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.204_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.204-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> A treatise known as the <i>Minnebüchlein</i> (<i>Little Book of Love</i>) is sometimes, but probably incorrectly, attributed to Suso.<sup id="cite_ref-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.204_9-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bernard_McGinn_2005_p.204-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Suso was very widely read in the later Middle Ages. There are 232 extant manuscripts of the <a href="/wiki/Middle_High_German" title="Middle High German">Middle High German</a> <i>Little Book of Eternal Wisdom</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> The Latin <a href="/wiki/Horologium_Sapientiae" title="Horologium Sapientiae"><i>Clock of Wisdom</i></a> was even more popular: over four hundred manuscripts in <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a>, and over two hundred manuscripts in various medieval translations (it was translated into eight languages, including Dutch, French, Italian, Swedish, Czech, and English). Many early printings survive as well. The <i>Clock</i> was therefore second only to the <i>Imitation of Christ</i> in popularity among spiritual writings of the later Middle Ages.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Among his many readers and admirers were <a href="/wiki/Thomas_%C3%A0_Kempis" title="Thomas à Kempis">Thomas à Kempis</a> and <a href="/wiki/John_Fisher" title="John Fisher">John Fisher</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wolfgang Wackernagel and others have called Suso a "<a href="/wiki/Minnesinger" class="mw-redirect" title="Minnesinger">Minnesinger</a> in prose and in the spiritual order" or a "Minnesinger of the Love of God" both for his use of images and themes from secular, courtly, romantic poetry and for his rich musical vocabulary.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> The mutual love of God and man which is his principal theme gives warmth and color to his style. He used the full and flexible <a href="/wiki/Alemannic_German" title="Alemannic German">Alemannic</a> idiom with rare skill, and contributed much to the formation of good German prose, especially by giving new shades of meaning to words employed to describe inner sensations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcMahon1913_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcMahon1913-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy_and_veneration">Legacy and veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_Suso&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Legacy and veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In the world Suso was esteemed as a preacher, and was heard in the cities and towns of <a href="/wiki/Swabia" title="Swabia">Swabia</a>, Switzerland, <a href="/wiki/Alsace" title="Alsace">Alsace</a>, and the Netherlands. His apostolate, however, was not with the masses, but rather with individuals of all classes who were drawn to him by his singularly attractive personality, and to whom he became a personal director in the spiritual life.
</p><p>Suso was reported to have established among the Friends of God a society which he called the Brotherhood of the Eternal Wisdom. The so-called <i>Rule of the Brotherhood of the Eternal Wisdom</i> is but a free translation of a chapter of his <i>Horologium Sapientiae</i> and did not make its appearance until the fifteenth century.
</p><p>Suso was <a href="/wiki/Beatification" title="Beatification">beatified</a> in 1831 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_XVI" title="Pope Gregory XVI">Pope Gregory XVI</a>, who assigned 2 March as his <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a>, celebrated within the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Order" title="Dominican Order">Dominican Order</a>. The Dominicans now celebrate his feast on 23 January, the <i><a href="/wiki/Feria" title="Feria">feria</a></i>, or "free" day, nearest the day of his death.
</p><p>The words of the Christmas song "<a href="/wiki/In_dulci_jubilo" title="In dulci jubilo">In dulci jubilo</a>" are attributed to Suso.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Editions_and_translations">Editions and translations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Henry_Suso&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Editions and translations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p><b><i>The Exemplar</i> (<a href="/wiki/Middle_High_German" title="Middle High German">Middle High German</a>):</b>
</p>
<ul><li>Henry Suso, <i>Das Buch von dem Diener (The Life of the Servant)</i>, ed. K. Bihlmeyer, <i>Heinrich Seuse. Deutsche Schriften</i>, 1907</li></ul>
<p>(translated by Frank Tobin, in <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=gCjpQIrqHmwC"><i>The Exemplar, with Two German Sermons</i></a>, New York: Paulist Press, 1989, pp.&#160;61–204)
</p>
<ul><li><i>Das Büchlein der ewigen Weisheit (The Little Book of Eternal Wisdom)</i>, ed. K. Bihlmeyer, ibid.</li></ul>
<p>(trans. in F. Tobin, ibid., pp.&#160;204–304)
</p>
<ul><li><i>Das Büchlein der Wahrheit (The Little Book of Truth)</i>, ed. K. Bihlmeyer, ibid.</li></ul>
<p>(trans. in F. Tobin, ibid., pp.&#160;305–332)
</p>
<ul><li><i>Das Briefbüchlein (The Little Book of Letters)</i>, ed. K. Bihlmeyer, ibid., pp.&#160;360–393</li></ul>
<p>(trans. in F. Tobin, ibid., pp.&#160;333–360)
</p>
<ul><li><i>"The exemplary life and writings of Blessed Henry Suso</i>, Complete ed. based on manuscripts, with a critical introd. &amp; explanatory notes by Nicholas Heller</li></ul>
<p>(translated from the German by Sister M. Ann Edward (Sister Mary of the Immaculate Heart). 2 v. (c) Priory Press; 15 Apr 1962)<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li><i>Exemplar, A complete and illustrated (bilingual) Dutch translation.<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.seusewerken.nl/"></a></i><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.seusewerken.nl/">Seusewerken.nl<i></i></a></li></ul>
<p>(translated from the Middle High German by Peter Freens; with illustrations by Anna Ruiters. 
</p><p><br />
</p><p><b>Preaching and Letters (Middle High German):</b>
</p>
<ul><li>Henry Suso, <i>The Great Book of Letters</i>, ed. K. Bihlmeyer, <i>Heinrich Seuse. Deutsche Schriften</i>, 1907, pp.&#160;405–494</li>
<li>Sermons 1 and 4 (those now recognized as authentic) are published in English translation in <i>The Exemplar, with Two German Sermons</i></li></ul>
<p>(trans. F. Tobin, (New York: Paulist Press, 1989), pp.&#160;361–376)
</p><p><b>Latin:</b>
</p>
<ul><li>Henry Suso, <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=T5if3b2zV-gC"><i>Horologium sapientiae (Clock of Wisdom)</i></a>, ed. P. Künzle, <i>Heinrich Seuses Horologium sapientiae</i>, Freiburg: Universitatsverlag, 1977</li></ul>
<p>(translated by Edmund Colledge, <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=gLzrOHs9aIIC"><i>Wisdom's Watch upon the Hours</i></a>, <a href="/wiki/Catholic_University_of_America_Press" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic University of America Press">Catholic University of America Press</a> [1994])
</p>
